One of these reactions, for example, is the one that created our Earth, and it is said that after many collisions of planets after the bang, the dust particles from the crashes created dust that formed our planet and in this process, certain molecules had to bond at a proper time, and the chances of this process having sucess are slim, so I think there is some outer influence that helped us out. God didn't make the Earth because if he did, then were in the bible does it talk about the dinosaurs or other creatures that are far older than what the bible claims to be the age of Earth. So there leaves many flaws in these statements on our planets creation,but we are not perfect! That is my view.

That argument is that you have an old book or a collection of texts that claim something to be true, correct? You might call this a straw-man, but keep in mind this is the only argument for a specific god, not just "an entity that created the universe", and are therefore perfectly valid. This IS what every religion says. Everything else is pretty much variations of ethics and opinions.There is not a shred of evidence but your precious texts that claim you to be right; and so far I've written down four different religions that all use the same goddamned argument for their religion to be right. Tell me; how can one religion be more right than another when that religion is using the exact same argument?!My point here is that the argument in itself is ridiculous and incredibly silly. I need you religious folk to understand how ridiculous the argument is. I'm not sure if there is any way to convince you that this argument is the dumbest argument ever invented; but I'll try, and hopefully some people who share my opinion will join in.Pick a book/series of text, what have you. I'll refer to it as a book because most religious texts are gathered in the form of a book as far as I know at this particular time. I want you to think about these things:1. Is the book coherent? Does it very simply and without confusing you tell you what it is about? If not, and there are a gajillion ways of interpreting it, you might want to reconsider that particular book.2. Does the book speak of magical things that go against the laws of physics (For the Modern Theist on the Go who has no time for looking up terms and so forth the rest of us have - a law: pretty much as "proven" as anything can be outside of a mathematical construct) that cannot be replicated (again; I feel I have to explain: If someone lights on fire when I point at them, and it just won't happen again, I can't really say I lit them on fire - coincidence is a more likely event). If these "magical things" are written down; and there is no proof but that they've been written down, you really might want to reconsider, seeing as "magical things" can be written down by anyone. Also - coincidences happen. Deal with it.3. Does the book comply with Occam's razor (Occam's razor; simple version: If you have various hypotheses {For the Modern Theist on the Go - a hypothesis: Ex: A ball falls down because God has attached invisible rubber-bands to it and the centre of the Earth. It is important to note that a hypothesis, unlike a theory, does not have to be supported by a larger magnitudes of evidence to suggest its truthfulness.} that have the same amount of evidence, pick the one that makes the least assumptions)? If not, you might want to reconsider, as jumping to conclusions is, as you know if you have common sense, very silly (If your hat is gone, it is unlikely that alien baseball-players from Jupiter have stolen it to pay their rent).4. Does the book constantly contradict itself? Example: "Thou shalt not kill." "And then the bears mauled the 42 children," - unless you're into hypocrisy, this isn't a book for you.5. Does the book make outrageous statements about certain minorities, ethnic or otherwise, that would have them discriminated if doctrine was followed? This one should be a no-brainer.6. Does the Holy Walrus of Greenland say the book is okay? If so, ignore the last 5 "rules" of finding a good, non-ridiculous religious book, because we all know the Holy Walrus of Greenland is always right. We know he's right because the book some people wrote says so, and who are we to contradict dead people whose intentions we know nothing of?

AalcedoniaNov 28 2008, 05:47 PM
Nazer' post='4444563 wrote on Nov 28 2008, 11:06 AM:
There are no "philosophical proofs" for "God". Rather the contrary. Logic and Occam's Razor say to presume upon "God's" existence is nothing short of a fallacy. Only if you are referring to non-interventionist Deism can you say it's logical for such a being to exist.
Incidentally, William of Ockham himself (a Catholic) didn't think that the famous "razor" disproved God's existence. His idea was more like what people have already said here, that belief in God is entirely a matter of faith. (Myself, I think Ockham was wrong on that count.) But in any case...Neither logic nor Occam's Razor say any such thing. First, logic is not a philosophical system, but simply the study of reasoning; any sort of reasoned argument uses logic. Logic in and of itself says nothing at all on the subject.Second, Occam's Razor is not an infallible guideline. It is a rule that states one should not introduce unnecessary plurality into an argument ("entia non sunt multiplicanda praeter necessitatem"): this does not mean that the theory which appears simplest is necessarily the best. Rather, it means that a theory should be free from unnecessary elements, which have nothing to do with the phenomenon being explained. Moreover, the assumption that the universe is caused by God is no more inherently complicated than the assumption that the universe is caused by nothing.

alcedonia' post='4444678 wrote on Nov 28 2008, 12:47 PM:
Incidentally, William of Ockham himself (a Catholic) didn't think that the famous "razor" disproved God's existence. His idea was more like what people have already said here, that belief in God is entirely a matter of faith. (Myself, I think Ockham was wrong on that count.) But in any case...Neither logic nor Occam's Razor say any such thing. First, logic is not a philosophical system, but simply the study of reasoning; any sort of reasoned argument uses logic. Logic in and of itself says nothing at all on the subject.Second, Occam's Razor is not an infallible guideline. It is a rule that states one should not introduce unnecessary plurality into an argument ("entia non sunt multiplicanda praeter necessitatem"): this does not mean that the theory which appears simplest is necessarily the best. Rather, it means that a theory should be free from unnecessary elements, which have nothing to do with the phenomenon being explained. Moreover, the assumption that the universe is caused by God is no more inherently complicated than the assumption that the universe is caused by nothing.
No, the simplest theory is always the best; even if that does not necessarily mean that it is true.For example, classical theories of gravity and motion as arrived at by Newton was both the simplest theory based on the evidence at the time, it was of course, not true, but in non-quantum or non-relativistic frames, it operated very well and served as an important tool, if I shot a cannon ball in a certain way, I can easily use F=ma to calculate the trajectory.Later on Einstein arrived at the theories of special and general relativity, showing that gravity and acceleration did not operate as Newton had suggested, and with investigations into quantum effects, it is now know that even the theory of relativity is not adequate in describing all physical phenomena.What is important is that F=ma was stated as it is, and not for example, xF=ma, where x is called, let’s say, god, and is set to be a constant of 1. According to Occam's razor, there is no need for the superfluous in stating the law. If F=ma is adequate, there is no need to inject god into the equation.As for god and nothing being equally complicated causes; well, that's just not true, you still have the job of defining god (how am I supposed to know what such a thing is if I don't believe in it?), whereas I think I can safely presume that we both are aware of what the concept of nothingness means. Besides, nothing is not necessarily the default position, why is “we don’t know” not an acceptable placeholder, while we investigate?As for the universe even having a "cause", that's another discussion in and of itself. Causation requires a linear, one way flow of time, which is, as far as we know, specific to the universe. Logically, it's quite possible that the universe could not have had a cause. Further considering the fact that time may just be due to the arrangement of the universe as a cascade of dimensions along a high to low entropy gradient...well, you get the point.

Doing a rez here.Since trancebam didn't really reply to my points yet I think I'll try to summarize the particular points I've responded to.God not existing is the parsimonious answer to an unfalsifiable question.Basically, there's no evidence for god existing, so there's no reason to act as if god exists, regardless of whether or not god exists.Another popular point has been the anthropic argument, which I've been arguing is a logical fallacy and answered by the argument from poor design.The wikipedia article on the existence of god is actually quite good, and makes many of the points already present in the thread, though it's more precise, accurate, and comprehensive:http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God_exists#Ar...t_belief_in_GodI rate as a strong atheist on the scale there.

338542788Apr 23 2009, 10:24 PM
reddeath26' post='4581298 wrote on Apr 23 2009, 04:54 PM:
I find it quite difficult to see situations where a person can be both agnostic and atheist. An agnostic person believing that we can not know whether God exists or not whereas an atheist believing there is no such thing as a God. Although as my avatar suggests, I take this from a more particularist/pluralistic perspecitve. Nonetheless I am interested in seeing how you came to your conclusion.
There are levels of atheism, it's not a binary state of "god exists" and "god does not exist". For example, you can say that god does not exist because the nonexistence of god is unfalsifiable, while not saying that god does not exist and can not possibly exist. There's also levels of agnosticism aside from "the existence of god(s) is knowable" or "the existence of god(s) is unknowable". For example you can say that the existence of certain gods is known to be false (Iesu isn't the son of god, Mohammad isn't his prophet, etc), while also saying that there are definitions of god(s) that are unfalsifiable, and thus unknowable. Because of this, it's possible for these beliefs to intersect depending on how one defines things. For example, the existence of a god that is unfalsifiable would be both unknowable and meaningless; an unknowable god would obviously fit with agnosticism, while a meaningless god can be interpreted to be equivalent to an nonexistent god.Technically, tenants of certain religions promote agnostic theism, while they also have vocal evidentialist apologists. So if you're really looking for a contradictions, it can probably be found more easily in religion.

38542788' post='4581322 wrote on Apr 24 2009, 10:24 AM:
For example, you can say that god does not exist because the nonexistence of god is unfalsifiable, while not saying that god does not exist and can not possibly exist.
I would like to see further development of this point here. If something does not exist, how can it be possible for it to also exist at the same time? If one states that an object does not exist, surely this is also ruling out the possibility of it existing.
38542788' post='4581322 wrote on Apr 24 2009, 10:24 AM:

338542788Apr 24 2009, 04:15 AM
reddeath26' post='4581381 wrote on Apr 23 2009, 08:31 PM:
I would like to see further development of this point here. If something does not exist, how can it be possible for it to also exist at the same time? If one states that an object does not exist, surely this is also ruling out the possibility of it existing.
Basically, that person would believe:"god exists" is falsifiable (by observing god, we can confirm that god exists)"god does not exist" is not (necessarily) falsifiable (not being able to observe god does not mean that god does not exist)However, for the sake of parsimony, god does not exist can be accepted in the case that "god exists" has not been falsified. This view would be a version of agnostic atheism. Basically, if there's no reason to expect that god exists, I'll live that way until I see evidence to the contrary, though I also accept that it whether some entity called god exists may be impossible to determine.Saying that god cannot possibly exist is stronger than that. Mostly this is due to paradoxes in attributes traditionally given to god, such as omniscience or omnipotence. This would be a non-agnostic atheistic position.It should be noted that in the first case, with the agnostic atheist, the unfalsifiable god bears pretty much no relation to deities in traditional religions. Because it's not falsifiable, its existence is basically irrelevant. The nonexistence of gods in various religions is falsifiable, by observing the contradictions between the claims of a particular religion and the real world.

I believe God is real. Several reasons. I'll enumerate...1. Experience. Through faith, we can experience God. Reading the bible will help us strengthen this faith. There are parts in the bible that are true in our life and in the world.2. Religion. There may be different religions but all of which has a God, or more, except the atheist. Like Buddha, Allah...there is the same God in different names in different religion. And believers of that religion all do good to please or follow their God.3. Supernatural beings... Or in other words, spirits. Some people can see or feel them but we can't give real or scientific explanation. Demons or ghosts, they are real. Where'd they come from? Maybe they may be related to afterlife. Have you read Dante Alighieri's work, the divine comedy and the inferno? If he didn't really went to hell/purgatory for real, then he must have a frighteningly creative imagination. How could he have thought of all those that he saw there? What he have wrote must have some inspiration. And that might me the real thing.4. First Cause. Aristotle's theory of first cause, that the cause of everything from the start is God. For example, the paper's cause is the tree, the tree's cause is the seed, the seed's cause the soil, the soil's cause is the earth, the earth's cause is the universe... the universe' cause... if science cannot give any explanation, that would be God.

It is very hard to imagine that as complex as life itself is that there isn't a creator behind it. Although people always argue about what god is, or whether god exists, you have to stop and ask yourself how is anything possible without a god? Does anyone know how complex the smallest things in the universe really are? Can anyone honestly say that randomness created the right circumstances for you to be sitting and reading this right now? The odds would be so unlikely that the very formation of basic life would be impossible. This isn't just a machine universe where all things happen due to randomness, mistakes, and coincidences. The universe is too complex and confusing for that to be true. I think most people forget and disbelieve in god because he... her... isn't always doing every little thing for us, but if god did everything for us and solved all our problems, what would the point of the life we live in now be anyway? It would be pretty boring if you ask me. Sure, most of us wish things could just be handed to us and that we could get instant results, and that there were no conflicts to resolve, but it wouldn't be as good if we were given everything and left with no new challenges to overcome. Toppling challenges make us stronger individuals, and that is part of the beauty of the life god has set for us. Think of our free will. That means we make some decisions for ourselves and change the world we live in by our own hands. That means if your life sucks, you have the power to change it, but only if you really want it to change. God won't baby you and cater to everything you want so don't even go there. So is god real or not real? God is god. That means he/she can do anything, be anything, and knows everything. If you can't see god as real you are looking at things the wrong way. People who try to disprove god can provide no proof that god doesn't exist because they are just dumbfounded as the rest of us by the great mysteries of how the universe truly works. Mr. So-and-so scientist said this is how it is so god must not exist. How could god exist if such terrible things are happening in the world? The thing is, no one can really speak for god because things are as they are because god wills them to be so, thats what being a god means... duh! But that doesn't mean god is to blame for bad things happening. If bad things happen because of another person it is their fault because they have free will and god isn't making any of the sins they commit happen. God may not always be visible, but our eyes don't always show us the truth. If god wants you to see or speak to him he would allow that. If god wanted you to be able to do things others saw as impossible, god would make it happen. If god wants things to happen a certain way, it will. Basically it is up to god, not the person, in what happens. God exists and that means he is in control, and not us. So don't say he isn't real just because you can't see him, hear him, or always get the results you want.

someone wrote:
Mr. So-and-so scientist said this is how it is so god must not exist.
First of all, science doesn't work that way. It isn't "some guy who calls himself a scientist says x, therefore x is true." There is an entire community of people who know a whole helluva lot more than you or I, and they run experiments and observe the world, and they can independently verify and disprove each other. Through observations and experimentation, we learn how the universe works, to the best of our knowledge.That's not to say, however, that the scientific method is the end-all be-all and everything we learn is set in stone. No, that's not the case. Everything we learn is subject to change as soon as something that contradicts what we would claim to know comes forth.Scientists have never said that God cannot possibly exist. Again, you're shifting the burden of proof. It is up to the person making the claim that God exists to provide proof that God exists.

someone wrote:
Why must you have proof of god? Did you ever stop and think of the possibility that evidence cannot be represented in this realm of existence? What counter-evidence do you have that makes god untrue?
Positive claims require positive evidence. When person A makes claim X, person A must then demonstrate that claim X is true. The default position for any and every claim is disbelief. Again, I bring up unicorns; Do you have anything that proves, definitely and incontrovertibly, that unicorns do not exist? No. And why don't you believe in them? Because there is no evidence for their existence. So why do you believe in God? Is it because of scripture?Now we get in to standards of evidence.Every claim requires a different standard. The claim, "My name is Geoff," for example, doesn't require much; Names are inconsequential (unless in specific cases of, "No you're not, I am!"). I'll believe you if you tell me your name is Geoff, or Fred, or Tod. Now, if your name was something like, "Gleexspack of Gilgoran," I'd ask for a birth certificate, or the legal forms for your name change.In the case of God, every claim attributed with God requires some extraordinary evidence. His existence, despite no physical manifestation, for example.This is probably better explained by Tracie's Fallacy Model, the invisible, supernatural, transcendental dice. This is from The Atheist Experience, a live call-in, public access cable television show in Austin Texas. I had no part in making this, but it illustrates the point here quite well. The video is in three parts, I'm sure you can find two and three from "Related Videos." http://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Kvftq2ystmY

Aanimay0Oct 12 2009, 09:13 PM
I believe with confidence (which is different than certainty, since I have not seen Him face-to-face, yet) that God is real. I also believe that I, and everybody else, can't completely understand God - that's nonsense! Of course, to say that I believe in God, I should try to describe what I mean by that word before I say why I think he exists.God is eternal - Nobody and nothing 'made' God. Always was, always will be. Outside of time.God is everywhere - That does NOT mean God is everyTHING. The physical world is not God, but God cannot be avoided by distance.God is all-powerful - All things are possible with God. some have said "Can God make a rock to big for him to lift?" to try and stump Theologians, since both 'yes' and 'no' are a limit to his power. This is an example of a poor question, because the question is nonsense. All THINGS are possible with God, nonsense is a non-thing.God is all-knowing - That one's pretty self-explanatory.God is good - I believe that our perception of good is slightly distorted from true goodness, so it is sometimes hard to see that God has no evil, but that God does good things is undeniable (if there was nothing good, how would you know there was something wrong to compare it to?) God is personal and living - This is a little harder to understand because he's infinite and we're finite, but I don't think personality (emotions, relationship, etc.) came from nowhere. And as the accepted cell theory states 'life comes from previous life, life does not come from non-life'I didn't create the world. So who did? God did! I think that part is obvious to everybody, but the nature of God is what we debate - is God impersonal force (maybe you call him Chance) or personal being, loving or indifferent, all-knowing or experimenting with the universe, etc.? these are the real questions. I believe in God, and I believe in science. They are not in conflict, and I think science helps to explain/confirm God's character. For instance, the second law of thermodynamics states that entropy (chaos) will increase over time in a closed system. How could the universe, especially earth, be so organized? Cell Theory - life cannot come from non-life. Where did life come from? I think God has always been living (though perhaps in a different way than we biologically classify life) and made life after he designed a world suitable for life. I think that personality (the soul, or perhaps it is sometimes called the Ego) is something that could not have just happened, so I see it as evidence of a God of personality, which means he also must have a plan and purpose for humanity. I don't want to keep going on forever, so let me close by saying that I understand what it is like to doubt this stuff, it is not simply obvious, and I'm not going to claim these are easy answers. Deep questions need deep answers, so if anybody wants to comment on one of the things I said because I was unclear or because you disagree or see a flaw in my reasoning (I'm not perfect) I'd love to discuss it. I hope I didn't seem demeaning to anybody and I respect your opinions!

I don't want to keep going on forever, so let me close by saying that I understand what it is like to doubt this stuff, it is not simply obvious, and I'm not going to claim these are easy answers. Deep questions need deep answers, so if anybody wants to comment on one of the things I said because I was unclear or because you disagree or see a flaw in my reasoning (I'm not perfect) I'd love to discuss it. I hope I didn't seem demeaning to anybody and I respect your opinions!
Arguments from personal incredulity, more bald assertions, and more stupidity. There's really no reason this post should fly in the debate section.

Your interpretations are prescriptive though, if you accept them you must act by them, they are also not parsimonious, in that many such interpretations can exist.In that case, if people accept contradictory non-parsimonious interpretations they can come into conflict without a reliable manner of resolving that conflict. The scientific conclusion is always parsimonious and as such it does not have multiple interpretations, it is also not prescriptive unless certain axioms are accepted (whereas in your case the axioms are tied to your interpretation).

338542788Oct 13 2009, 04:05 PM
hama4' post='4719315 wrote on Oct 13 2009, 11:11 AM:
I don't understand why are my belief contradictory that is the point I said religion doesn't contradict science it just take a different view of the matter returning to the lightning example my interpretation doesn't say that lightning isn't a discharge of electricity it just further philosophically interpret it. and why is it parsimonious?
There are multiple non-contradictory interpretations, one similarity is that none of them are parsimonious because they all add to the parsimonious conclusion (which would be the scientific conclusion). For example, your religion might say that lightening represents god's anger, another might say that lightening is god smiling at us, another might say that lightening is god's metaphorical ejaculate, they each differ and they each prescribe a particular mode of behavior (if they don't then those interpretations would be essentially worthless). Those prescribed modes of behavior are contradictory, which means that using your methodology, we have arrived at contradictory results, this is logically unsound, so your methodology of assigning divine interpretations to natural phenomena is unsound, which means that use of such of methodology should be rejected.

hama4' post='4720880 wrote on Oct 15 2009, 01:21 PM:
" For example, the time you spent praying while you were dying would obviously have been pointless if god didn't exist. Why are you going off a completely different tangent?My point was to illustrate the importance of being right, it didn't directly have anything to do with whether god exists, it just says that there are serious consequences to thinking that god exists so you should be careful before adopting such a position.I'm not contesting whether god exists in that statement, I'm contesting your claim that believing in god has no consequences in this world.For example, the statement "I should try to kill everyone I meet today" does not contradict empirical observations that can be made about the world. However, our observations about the world can still allow us to judge whether such a thing is "good" or "bad". For example, I can observe that I enjoy being alive, and applying some logic to the situation I can say that I probably shouldn't kill everyone I meet today because that's unlikely to end up well for me. In a similar manner, the statement "god exist" and "I should do good things as defined by god", also have consequences because they prescribe particular behaviors, for example, someone may wish to have an abortion because "I don't want to go through pregnancy at this time", so in that case and abortion would be "good", if instead you accept that abortion is a sin, having an abortion would obviously be "bad", in this case, the person's religious beliefs may change her behavior if she thinks that having an abortion is more "bad" than "good". This is why it's important to be right, because we all base our actions on such moral calculus, we do things that are most good or least bad, if we allow belief in god to influence our behavior we should be sure that the belief in god is correct and justified.

I'll try to help you out here and point out the problem.The problem is with axiom 5, "evil isn't stopped".Here it's important to define what is "good" and what is "bad" (or evil, in this case). Most religions claim divine inspiration for their moral codes, for example, homosexuality is bad because god said so. In such a sense, morality is derived from god. If that is the case and we accept axiom 1 (god is good), than any action by good is automatically good. God could torture me for eternity and it would be good. God could lie to his believers and grind your soul to dust and be good. God can destroy the world on a whim tomorrow and still be good. After all, if the moral code that you act by is "good" because it came from god, it follows that events in the corporal world and whatever worlds may follow must also be "good".The obvious problem here is that "god is good" is a lie. I do not mean to say that god is evil (regardless of whether god exists or not), I'm saying that the religious do not derive their morality from god anymore than the atheist, you do not believe that god is good. Those who claim to follow a moral code given by god still somehow behave in widely different ways, it's not just that they follow the same code to varying degrees, it's that the code itself is different. For example, the view that abortion or homosexuality is a sin is not accepted by all Christians. If different groups of Christians each claim to follow a moral code given by god, why is moral code that they actually subscribe to different? The obvious answer is that morality is a complex issue that is not primarily influenced by religion, we don't stone homosexuals to death in the US, but it can certainly be found in other cultures with an equal claim to belief in god.
hama4' post='4720880 wrote on Oct 15 2009, 01:21 PM:
anyway I wrote my arguments just to say that their is no scientific or philosophic reason to not believe in god. So I am free to assume that god exist as it is the same probability that he doesn't
Of course there is, if you assign god qualities they can be tested, for example, that was the entire point of the question of evil. Statement: "god is good" and "god is responsible for everything that happens in the world", observation: "evil occurs in the world", conclusion: "god is not good or does not exist". There, I've just used logic to disprove the existence of a good god (note that you don't have to accept the argument itself, I'm just saying that there's reason to argue against the existence of any god to which qualities can be assigned.)If you define god as to be so vague that he's unfalsifiable, he can then be eliminated by application of parsimony.

someone wrote:
What you are talking about is called agnosticism.
No, I'm not, and here's the difference; atheism and theism are labels that provide what you believe, while gnosticism and agnosticism are labels that refer to what you claim to know or not know. "I'm agnostic" is not an answer to "What do you believe?" While it's true that I won't claim to an absolute certainty that there is no God, I find the idea highly unlikely, and so with any use of a colloquial definition of the word "know," I would claim to know that there is no God. This is not a position held by every, or even most, atheists, however.

peoples peopleton' post='4768774 wrote on Dec 30 2009, 09:43 AM:

I'd like to take this opportunity to go over a couple definitions right quick. First of all, agnosticism/gnosticism are relevant to claims of knowledge. In this respect, you, as an 'agnostic,' do not claim to know whether or not there is a God. That's fine and well, but it doesn't tell us what you believe. Theism and atheism describe your belief or lack thereof. From your statements, you do not believe in any Gods, and you are therefore an atheist. An agnostic atheist, but atheist nonetheless.While the absence of evidence is not evidence of absence, it sure as hell does nothing to support the idea of a God. You see, there's this thing called the burden of proof. Positive claims require positive evidence. If I make the claim that there is a God, I have to meet a standard of evidence in order to make that a justified belief. For instance, I feel justified in believing that gravity is what keeps me on the ground, and not just angels pulling me down when I jump.Nothing has actually met the standard of evidence required to support a belief in a God, and in fact most definitions of "God" lead to internal contradiction (see: the problem of evil), or the God claimed is wholly irrelevant (transcendent and has no physical manifestation), and so not only do I feel justified in rejecting the claims of Gods, at the least the ones I've come across, I feel justified in the belief that there are no Gods.That said, your personal belief that no religion has it right, well, congrats on your unfalsifiable position. I will only say that you have no reason to believe (or claim to know) that's the case.

Right away there are logical problems with the person's argument. They state:
someone wrote:
This paper argues that at least one of the following propositions is true: (1) the human species is very likely to go extinct before reaching a “posthuman” stage; (2) any posthuman civilization is extremely unlikely to run a significant number of simulations of their evolutionary history (or variations thereof); (3) we are almost certainly living in a computer simulation. A number of other consequences of this result are also discussed.
You can't argue that we are unlikely to reach a post human phase of evolution and then argue that we are in a simulation created by a population composed of people belonging to a post human stage of evolution.

someone wrote:
Besides, I actually do believe in some kind of evolution, just not the same that you belive in. I do belive, though, that human bodies tend to change according to their enviroment. That's not really how humans work. For instance, I grew up in a small town in the desert in California, where it was pretty hot year-round. However, when I go to the mountains to ski, I don't freeze to death. Evolution doesn't mean that my physiology changes because of where I live. Evolution is a broad term for the specific changes made to a species over time, through the process of natural selection driving shifts in dominant genes across generations.When you drag "God" into the mix, and claim that God will make someone from the mountains unable to survive the heat of the desert, you're so far off the mark of evolution that using that label is just downright silly. When you drag your theology into physiological changes by way of climate, you're so far from the scientific meaning of the word "evolution," you might as well just stop talking, because all you're doing at that point is demonstrating just how little you actually understand the subject.

someone wrote:
If God is omniscient, it means he knows what we perceive as the future. That is so shallow and depthless.Well, a wise bald man also said once that a little philosophy, can lead to atheism. However, a whole load of philosophy can lead to believing in God. So you gave your little philosophy and now it's my turn.Our actions indeed ARE predetermined. But this predetermination has gone according to the actions that God knows we will have done anyway. God knows what happened, what's happening, what will happen, and if something didn't happen, He knows how it would have happened.I remember in school, my friends were all like, so we can't change fate? So if like somebody was in trouble and they were supposed to get killed tomorrow and we pray for them, we can't change it?Actually, God knows you're GOING to pray for them, therefore, fate was already altered before you were even created so that he would be saved from getting killed because you prayed for them. If you hadn't prayed, they could have gotten killed and things would have went like they were supposed to. (in case you're thinking about it, please don't argue about the praying part, because this is just an example).Imagine it a little like this. You told me you were going to catch the bus at 7:30 a.m. tomorrow. So I now know when you're going to catch the bus. However, me knowing you're going to catch the bus at that time, doesn't mean you can't change it, or maybe not even do so at all. Me knowing, and you catching the bus are two independent events. Same thing with God. Him knowing, and you doing whatever you want to do are two independent events. You're doing whatever you want, He just knows what it is.Some would argue, well if God already knows all we're going to do, and whether we'll end up in hell or heaven, then what's the point? Why not just throw us right where we were supposed to end up and save us and Him the trouble?God is fair. Yes, He knows where I'll end up. Imagine if it's hell (because it could be). Imagine he threw me right there straight away. I'll be like 'WTH, you didn't even give me a chance! Maybe I would have ended up differently'. However, if he let me live this life, then threw me in hell, I'll know I deserved it. I'll have no argument to defend myself because I've lived my life but I didn't live it right, and my sins outweighed my deeds.Let me also add that I believe God is timeless. He is outside time. Our lives, past present and future happen in an instant to Him. God does timelessly know what happens in the future, but this knowledge is not causal. God sees our future free actions, but what He sees is the result of our freedom - God doesn't cause us to act in a particular manner.

someone wrote:
Richard Feynman, a Nobel Prize winner for quantum electrodynamics, said, "Why nature is mathematical is a mystery...The fact that there are rules at all is a kind of miracle."
(Richard Feynman, The Meaning of It All: Thoughts of a Citizen-Scientist (New York: BasicBooks, 1998), 43.)It's a "mystery".Need I say more?Still not convinced? Well, there are many recorded arguments. I found a few on a website so I'll copy and paste them:The Argument From DesignWhen we look at anything that has a design, we rightly conclude that it had a designer. Somebody designed chairs for a purpose. Somebody designed a glove to fit over a hand. The universe, and particularly human beings, show an unbelievable amount of design. This points to a Designer.The Argument From AnthropologyHumans have a complex personality. It seems (and is) absurd that such complexity could have emerged from nothing, with no cause. Rather, the human personality would need to be made by a Being with the same, if not greater, complex personality.The Argument From Cause-EffectEverything we can think of, and which we encounter, has been caused by something. Every effect has a cause. In something as complex and awesome as the universe, there is no difference. The Big Bang Theory does not destroy this argument, for no-one can explain the cause of the Big Bang.The Argument From OntologyIf there is a concept of something, this implies that the something does exist. For example, there is the popular concept of 'true love'. Although this concept might often seem elusive, the fact that we have the concept of true love in our heads, points to the real probability of the existence of true love.The Argument From MoralityThe sense of morals which most people have 'built-in', points to the existence of a moral Maker. More than this, the other option of evolution, has no way of explaining how morals came from pond-slime, and ultimately, from nothing.Oh, I could go on and on about why I believe God exists, but I think this is enough for now.

someone wrote:
If emotional isn't enough, and it probably isn't, let me carry on a little.(Robert Jastrow; "Message from Professor Robert Jastrow"; LeaderU.com; 2002.)What you believe in apparently has no explanation.(Richard Feynman, The Meaning of It All: Thoughts of a Citizen-Scientist (New York: BasicBooks, 1998), 43.)It's a "mystery".Need I say more?
We don't have an explanation for something? What a shocker. Even so, the fact that we don't know something is not evidence of a God. Early humans couldn't explain thunder as anything but God's wrath, and of course, we know now that it's sound produced by lightning breaking the sound barrier. Just because you can put the word "God" somewhere to try to explain something doesn't make it true. That's called a "God of the Gaps" argument.

someone wrote:
Wow, so the universe really did just flash into existence? How amazing and utterly believable. Way more believable than the somebody carefully designed it. Yeah, definitely.
Well, yes and no. The universe, as we know it today, has not always existed in the form it does today. The big bang theory states how the universe formed into the current state, but there's not really a mainstream model of what the universe was before that. And why is that? Because we have no evidence to suggest what it was before the big bang. There are several ideas about what it could have been, but they are untestable, and have no real evidence to support them, so they're hypotheses that remain such. It becomes especially complicated, and impossible to determine, when you factor in that the laws of physics and nature that we understand now could have been wildly different before the universe formed the state it's in now.Rejecting your assertion that a God created the universe is not an acceptance of the idea that it just "flashed into existence." Rejecting your assertion is not the acceptance of anything else. It is simply a rejection of a claim, not a belief in the opposite of that claim.

And now we get the point I've been trying to get you to make since I posted. The core reason you believe. Faith. But before we dive into that monster of a subject, the trust in someone. If I trust someone, it's because they've done something to make me trust them. Sure, they could be someone pretending, or something could happen down the line. But until I have any reason to doubt their intentions, or until something happens to make me mistrustful, they have my trust, because all of the evidence I have (my observations of this person) would point me towards trusting them. Again, at the point where I find suspicion in their actions, or some event occurs that breaks that trust between us, then I would stop trusting the person. And even until that point, I'm not completely trusting someone without good reason to. I might trust a person to keep a secret after a few weeks of getting to know them, but would I "completely" trust them? No. And that's where faith differs, and where you've fallen into the equivocation fallacy.Faith, in terms of religion, is belief in unjustified, unevidenced assertions. Attempting to justify religious faith by appealing to a more justifiable claim, which is itself justified by evidence and reason, and ignoring the justification in order to assume no justification is needed, is not a cogent argument.

someone wrote:

I'm no cosmologist, nor am I an evolutionary biologist, so explaining all of the intricacies of the two theories are beyond my scope. I could type out a rudimentary knowledge of the two, though it's not pertinent. The two theories are separate from whether or not a God exists, and if I could convince you of their validity (which I doubt), it wouldn't disprove God.Now, we come to where the burden of proof starts to matter. Any and every claim must meet a standard of evidence before it is accepted as true, and until that standard of evidence is met, the default position is rejection of that claim. Otherwise, we would be accepting every contradictory claim there is, such as "This is a basketball" and "This is not a basketball" for the same object. That's not to say that the default position is to believe the opposite of the claim, however; if someone claims that a bag is made of polyester, and another person claims it's made of cotton, the burden of proof dictates that we believe neither claim until it is demonstrated to be true. Now, in the case of a polyester or cotton bag, the truth is almost not worth the effort, but in the case of a God, it matters a great deal.Positive claims require positive evidence to support the claim. If I claim to have a dragon in my garage, then I must provide evidence of that, otherwise your belief of that claim is not justified. You do not have to provide evidence to the contrary in order to reject the claim.Do you have evidence that my dragon does not exist? What about my unicorn? Or the leprechaun that rides him? Or my garden full of fairies? Or the nymph that lives in my tree? Have you anything to disprove those? Because if you don't, then under your own logic, you must belief they exist until someone proves they don't. Or, you can go off of basic logic, and say that I probably don't have any of those things.What you've done with your belief in God is, you've thrown logic out of the window, in favor of believing something for which you have no good reason, and then use the word faith as a permission slip to do so. If you called it anything other than God, you'd be considered insane by most people, but because you've labeled it with the word "God," it's suddenly acceptable to most people, when it absolutely shouldn't be.

I could go through this definition, bit by bit, and explain why I think your God is unlikely to exist, but the fact of the matter is that the burden of proof still rests on you to actually provide evidence that this now-defined God exists.I will go ahead and ask a couple of questions, though. Something to ponder over. From the top of its IronChariots page (which goes into much greater detail):Suppose we have the following four premises: God is omnipotent. God is omnibenevolent. God is omniscient. Evil exists. We get the following contradiction. If God is omnibenevolent, then he does not want evil to exist. If God is omniscient, then he must know about all evil in the world. If God is omnipotent, then he must be capable of doing something about it. Therefore, evil should not exist. Dropping any one of those four premises would resolve the contradiction, but dropping #4 would require us to fundamentally redefine evil in some way, and dropping the other three would undermine the Christian concept of God.
someone wrote:
I partly agree. Because some things are just black and white with no third option. Sometimes it's just either or.
The only true dichotomy is a comparison of "x" and "not x." For existence, this means "God exists" and "God does not exist." While this is a true dichotomy, in that there are only two possibilities, likelihood becomes a factor. Given that I have no evidence of, nor have been convinced of, your God's existence, I find it unlikely that he exists. I have also seen no evidence to the contrary, but as explained earlier, the burden of proof justifies the belief that your God does not exist. This is a rejection of your claim, not a claim of the opposite.

We get the following contradiction. If God is omnibenevolent, then he does not want evil to exist. If God is omniscient, then he must know about all evil in the world. Rreddeath26May 01 2012, 10:50 AM
LightOfTheSacredFlames' timestamp='1335116516' post='5010113 wrote:

Im sorry but i still cant why you guys wont admit that god is real look i dont want to say too much but there are a lot of proofs about god being real but you have to be muslim and read the holy quran ok? im not gonna answer again until you have read the holy quran and you probably wont and for those who say that islam is not the correct religion then go see how many ppl from many religions have eventually became muslim

As you yourself have identified, Islam is but one of the many different religions which exist in our world. Cross culturally and historically there have been many different cultural understandings our ourselves, the 'world' we find ourselves in, and how we relate to it. One could so easily pick one of these cultural perspectives and question how it is that you can deny it's truthfulness. Furthermore your point on diffusion leading to the validation of a cultural belief is over simplistic and misleading at best. Ethnographies looking into diffusion have demonstrated that the process is not so one sided as you propose. Rather they interpret the incoming cultural beliefs from the standpoint of their current one's and at times will modify the incoming one. Furthermore you deny such complexities as political factors and historical circumstances. Such things do not happen in a vacuum.
